860	\subsection{Binary Detector Baselines for Ordinal Classification}
861	\label{app:detector_baselines}
862	
863	We evaluate whether existing binary AI-text detectors and membership inference methods, when repurposed for ordinal classification, can match our 19-feature RF pipeline.
864	
865	\paragraph{Binary detectors (Pythia nucleus).}
866	Binoculars~\cite{binoculars2024} and Fast-DetectGPT~\cite{fast_detectgpt2024} scores are computed on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) subset and fed to: (a)~a log-likelihood rank classifier (Log-Rank, 64.0\%), (b)~a combined RF trained on both detector scores (Combined, 74.2\%), and (c)~the standard 19-feature RF (78.7\%).
867	The 4.5\,pp gap (74.2\% vs.\ 78.7\%) confirms that perplexity distributional features capture ordinal depth structure beyond what generic detection scores provide.
868	Note that Fast-DetectGPT exhibits numerical overflow on Pythia nucleus d1 (mean perturbation score $>$28{,}000); results for this cell should be interpreted cautiously.
869	Across all 9 atlas cells, Binoculars and Fast-DetectGPT ordinal accuracy averages ${\sim}$35\% and ${\sim}$32\%, respectively.
870	
871	\paragraph{Membership inference baselines (all 9 cells).}
872	DetectGPT~\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt} (gpt2-large scoring model, t5-large perturbation model, 5 perturbations) achieves 30.7\% mean 4-class accuracy (d1--d3 BalAcc: 24.3\%).
873	Min-K\% Prob~\cite{shi2024_minkprob} ($K{=}20$) with CORAL ordinal calibration reaches 33.2\% (d1--d3 BalAcc: 40.4\%); the best single cell is gpt2xl\_temp09 at 50.6\%.
874	Min-K\%'s advantage over DetectGPT (+2.5\,pp) is consistent with token-level log-probabilities containing more depth-relevant information than perturbation-based curvature, but both remain within 10\,pp of chance.
875	CORAL calibration improves ordinal structure (d1--d3 BalAcc 40.4\% vs.\ 33.3\% random) without closing the gap to RF (71.6\%), confirming that the bottleneck is the input signal rather than the calibration strategy.
876	Perturbation-based and membership-inference methods (28--33\%) confirm that token-level detection scores lack ordinal depth resolution.
877	Cross-perplexity (Binoculars) is a notable exception: when evaluated across all 9 atlas cells with threshold calibration, Binoculars achieves 69.8\%; with multi-feature RF calibration (11 cross-perplexity statistics), it reaches 78.1\%, matching our 19-feature pipeline within 0.3\,pp (\S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}).
878	
879	\paragraph{Augmented feature set.}
880	To test whether detection scores carry complementary ordinal signal, we add Min-K\% probability ($K{=}20$) and DetectGPT perturbation discrepancy as additional RF features.
881	The 20-feature model (19 + Min-K\%) achieves 78.90\% mean 4-class accuracy (+0.48\,pp) and 72.20\% d1--d3 balanced accuracy (+0.60\,pp).
882	Min-K\% ranks \#3 by feature importance (0.095), behind \texttt{p90\_ppl} (0.161) and \texttt{var\_surprisal} (0.126); DetectGPT ranks \#17 (0.026).
883	The marginal improvement is consistent across 8 of 9 atlas cells, indicating that Min-K\%'s token-level log-probability signal provides weak but real complementary information to the passage-level distributional features.
884	The top-2 features (\texttt{p90\_ppl} + \texttt{var\_surprisal}) retain combined importance of 28.7\%, confirming that perplexity tail statistics remain the dominant depth markers even after augmentation.
885	
886	\subsection{Binoculars Feature Ablation and Signal Decomposition}
887	\label{app:feature_ablation_bino}
888	
889	To quantify the overlap and complementarity between our 19-feature pipeline and Binoculars' cross-perplexity features, we evaluate four feature configurations across all 9 atlas cells (Table~\ref{tab:bino_ablation}).
890	
891	\begin{table}[h]
892	\centering
893	\small
894	\caption{Feature ablation: overlap and merged configurations (9-cell mean, 5-fold CV, RF 200 trees). The 6 overlap features are the perplexity statistics shared between both pipelines (mean, variance, median, p10, p90, skewness).}
895	\label{tab:bino_ablation}
896	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
897	\toprule
898	Configuration & \#Feat. & Acc (\%) & $\Delta$ vs.\ 19-feat \\
899	\midrule
900	6 overlap only & 6 & 75.9 & $-$2.5 \\
901	Our 19 features & 19 & 78.4 & --- \\
902	Binoculars 11 features & 11 & 78.1 & $-$0.3 \\
903	Merged (19 + 11) & 30 & \textbf{82.6} & +4.2 \\
904	\bottomrule
905	\end{tabular}
906	\end{table}
907	
908	The 6 shared perplexity features alone achieve 75.9\% (97\% of the full pipeline's accuracy), confirming that mean-level perplexity distributional displacement is the core depth signal.
909	Our 13 additional features (kurtosis, surprisal variance, lexical/n-gram statistics) contribute +2.5\,pp of shape-level information; Binoculars' 5 unique features (observer-performer agreement score, cross-entropy statistics) contribute +4.2\,pp of cross-model divergence when merged.
910	
911	Table~\ref{tab:bino_ablation_percell} reports per-cell results for three configurations with per-cell breakdown (Bino-11 per-cell values are omitted because features were computed on the pooled 9-cell corpus).

Bino-11 per-cell values omitted because Binoculars features were computed on the pooled 9-cell corpus; the per-cell mean (78.1\%) is reported in Table~\ref{tab:bino_ablation}.}

923	Pythia nucleus & 76.7 & 78.7 & --- & 83.5 & +4.8 \\
924	Pythia temp09 & 73.9 & 76.8 & --- & 81.5 & +4.7 \\

928	OLMo top-$k$50 & 72.5 & 74.4 & --- & 78.4 & +4.0 \\

932	\midrule

939	The merged improvement varies by decoding strategy: nucleus and temperature cells gain +4.7\,pp on average, while top-k cells gain +3.2\,pp.

578:nucleus is monotonically increasing (d0v1=0.643 $<$ d1v2=0.713 $<$ d2v3=0.728);
579:temperature exhibits d2v3 collapse (0.618, the weakest boundary) because temperature scaling uniformly amplifies logit differences, accelerating convergence to the attractor distribution at deeper depths;
580:top-$k$ exhibits d0v1 collapse (0.561, the weakest boundary) because vocabulary truncation constrains d0 and d1 distributions to the same top-$k$ tokens, compressing their statistical distance.
594:\caption{14B cross-reference control: Pythia-1.4B reference vs.\ self-reference on Qwen2.5-14B text. $\Delta$: cross $-$ self. $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ computed from consecutive pairwise AUC.}
599:Strategy & Self-ref (\%) & Cross-ref (\%) & $\Delta$ & $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ & d0v1 AUC \\
610:Cross-reference accuracy drops by 6.8\,pp on average (range 4.2--9.1\,pp), but $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ \emph{rises} from 0 to 2 for nucleus and temperature: the consecutive d0v1 (0.70--0.74) and d1v2 (0.72--0.73) AUCs now exceed the 0.70 threshold, while d2v3 remains below (0.54--0.61).
773:The pairwise AUC profile differs from base models: d0v1 = 0.653, d1v2 = 0.620, d2v3 = 0.664, with a gap-dependent pattern (non-adjacent pairs yield higher AUC, e.g., d0v3 = 0.902) but no pair reaching the 0.75 threshold.
977:\caption{Prompted generation (GPT-2~XL, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, topic-seeded). 5-fold CV accuracy and pairwise AUC. Chance = 25\%.}
987:AUC d0v1 & 0.916 \\
988:AUC d1v2 & 0.782 \\
989:AUC d2v3 & 0.744 \\
999:Nevertheless, accuracy remains $2.8\times$ chance and pairwise AUC decays monotonically (0.916, 0.782, 0.744); the depth signal is therefore not an artifact of unconstrained generation.

        "content": "\\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}\n\\label{sec:atlas}\n\nWe present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.\nThe atlas spans $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells at the 1--1.5B parameter scale, evaluated at depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2, 3\\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}\n\\label{sec:atlas:id}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.\nAccuracy ranges from 68.9\\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.9\\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature) four-class (d1--d3 balanced: 58.9--81.5\\%), all exceeding the 25\\%/33\\% chance baselines.\nTwo axis effects emerge.\nGPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (75.8--85.9\\%), followed by OLMo (74.4--82.3\\%) and Pythia (68.9--78.7\\%).\nTop-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.\nThe mean within-model strategy gap is 9.3~pp, comparable to the 7.2~pp mean within-strategy model gap; decoding strategy contributes at least as much as model family to performance variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\\%) with bootstrap 95\\% CI (${\\pm}$half-width; $B{=}10{,}000$). RF with 200 trees, evaluated via 5-fold stratified CV (seed=42). Parenthesized: d1--d3 balanced accuracy from the same 4-class RF (chance = 25\\% / 33\\%). Depth-0 recall exceeds 97\\% in all cells. Within-distribution ordinal quality: mean absolute error (MAE)\\,=\\,0.23, QWK\\,=\\,0.89 (QWK$_{\\text{d1--d3}}$\\,=\\,0.74); 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$). $^\\dagger$Self-reference scoring (Pythia-1.4B is both generator and reference model); 4--11\\,pp advantage over cross-model references (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}). Cross-cell comparisons involving Pythia should be interpreted with this caveat.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& Nucleus & Temp.\\ $\\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B$^\\dagger$  & 78.7{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.5}\\,(71.8) & 76.8{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.5}\\,(69.3) & 68.9{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.6}\\,(58.9) \\\\\nOLMo-1B     & 78.9{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.5}\\,(72.7) & 82.3{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.5}\\,(76.6) & 74.4{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.5}\\,(66.2) \\\\\nGPT-2~XL    & \\textbf{84.3}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.5}\\,(79.6) & \\textbf{85.9}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.5}\\,(81.5) & \\textbf{75.8}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.6}\\,(68.1) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-class recall.}\nDepth-0 recall exceeds 97\\% in all 9 cells.\nAmong generated depths, d2 consistently yields the lowest recall (44--73\\%), reflecting its position at the center of the depth gradient where features overlap with both d1 and d3, consistent with d2-vs-d3 as the EDD bottleneck (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}; full per-class recall in Appendix~\\ref{app:per_class}).\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Feature Consistency Across the Grid}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nMulticollinearity is substantial (VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 of 19 features); importance rankings below indicate feature \\emph{family} contributions rather than individual effects; permutation importance (Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}) partially mitigates collinearity bias \\cite{strobl2008_conditional,hooker2021_permutation}.\nDespite the 17.1~pp accuracy spread across cells, the perplexity tail statistics family (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) dominate depth discrimination in every setting.\nWithin this family, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} are consistently prominent, though individual rankings should be interpreted cautiously given VIF${>}$10; group-level ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}, below) provides the collinearity-robust confirmation.\\footnote{The group-level dominance of perplexity tail statistics is robust across importance measures (Gini, SHAP, permutation; Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$).}\nConditional permutation importance \\citep{strobl2008_conditional} across all nine cells confirms \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} as the top-2 features in 8/9 cells (average bootstrap rank 1.4 and 1.7; CI width $\\leq 0.5$), consistent with Gini-based rankings despite VIF $> 10$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:conditional_pi}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking; Figure~\\ref{fig:atlas_heatmap} visualizes the importance landscape across all nine cells.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ ($[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information despite differing in absolute accuracy.\nTop-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$), as vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape at the cost of weaker signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nFeature ablation confirms this concentration: the top-5 features achieve 97.6\\% of full accuracy (76.5\\% vs.\\ 78.4\\%); group-level ablation shows perplexity features alone reach 76.6\\%, surprisal 73.2\\%, lexical diversity ${\\leq}$47\\%, and repetition ${\\leq}$40\\%.\\footnote{Best single feature (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) achieves 58.3\\%, providing a more competitive single-feature baseline than mean perplexity (25.1\\%).}\n\nThe feature hierarchy shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by Gini importance. \\textbf{Bold}: top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} 8/9 under SHAP (7/9 under Gini). Individual rankings are approximate due to multicollinearity (VIF${>}$10 for 14--16/19 features).}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (Gini importance, ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc.       & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.15)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), var\\_ppl\\,(.08), p75\\,(.07) \\\\\nPythia, $\\tau$     & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.14)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), p75\\,(.07), m\\_surp\\,(.07) \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$    & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.16)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.12)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), var\\_ppl\\,(.07), m\\_surp\\,(.06) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, nuc.    & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.21)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.21)}, p75\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.07), m\\_ppl\\,(.06) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, $\\tau$  & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.25)}, \\textbf{p75\\,(.15)}, m\\_surp\\,(.13), var\\_surp\\,(.12), p50\\,(.05) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, top-$k$ & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.24)}, \\textbf{p75\\,(.12)}, var\\_surp\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.09), ttr\\,(.04) \\\\\nOLMo, nuc.         & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.18)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.15)}, p75\\,(.11), m\\_surp\\,(.07), ttr\\,(.06) \\\\\nOLMo, $\\tau$       & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.21)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.16)}, p75\\,(.12), m\\_surp\\,(.09), ttr\\,(.05) \\\\\nOLMo, top-$k$      & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.20)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, p75\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.07), m\\_ppl\\,(.05) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap for the top-7 features. Rows: features ranked by mean Gini importance; columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Stars mark the two universal top-2 features (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}). Full 19-feature breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_ablation}.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}\n\nDepth features exhibit strong monotonic trends (Spearman $\\rho = -1.0$ for perplexity tail statistics; Figure~\\ref{fig:feature_trajectory}); inter-depth distances are non-uniform (d0-vs-d1 AUC ${>}$0.99 in all 9 cells, d2-vs-d3: 0.69--0.89).\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay and cross-model convergence.}\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically from $0.999$ (d0v1) to $0.683$ (d4v5); AUC-excess contraction ratios (0.80 at d1v2$\\to$d2v3, 0.69 at d3v4$\\to$d4v5) show convergence toward indistinguishability accelerates with depth (Figure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay}).\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near $T{=}0.75$ for all three models (Pythia $0.784$, OLMo $0.765$, GPT-2~XL $0.750$), while d4-vs-d5 converges to a narrow band ($0.681$--$0.684$, range $0.003$), suggesting an architecture-independent depth ceiling within the 1--1.5B nucleus setting.\nBootstrap testing ($H_0{:}\\, \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$; Bonferroni-corrected for 3 comparisons, $\\alpha{=}0.0167$) yields $p = 0.001$ (Pythia), $0.091$ (OLMo), $0.505$ (GPT-2~XL).\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nAcross the 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3), all cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ at $T{=}0.65$; at $T{=}0.70$, 8/9 cells; at $T{=}0.75$, 7/9; at $T{=}0.80$, 5/9.\nTop-$k$ configurations lose $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$); d2-vs-d3 is the consistent bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{AUC decay curves for Pythia-1.4B (nucleus, depths 0--5) under three pipeline configurations: binary pairwise RF, 4-class RF, and 6-class RF. Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories.\nTemperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$) yields the highest accuracy in 2/3 models (GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%, OLMo 82.3\\%) because uniform logit scaling accelerates convergence to an attractor~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the fingerprint but creating a boundary reversal risk under cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) produces the lowest accuracy (68.9--75.8\\%) because hard vocabulary truncation removes depth-discriminative low-probability tokens.\nThe strategy effect (9.3\\,pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.2\\,pp); deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy matters at least as much as the generator model.\n",
        "numLines": 123,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 123
      }
    },

  },

Prior z-score recovery (0.13→0.68) was with LR, not RF.\"}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"notes\": \"Z-score standardization has negligible effect on RF cross-strategy LOO. temp09 d1v2 AUC remains inverted: GPT-2 XL 0.1288→0.1348, OLMo 0.2529→0.2497, Pythia 0.576→0.594. Root cause: RF is a tree-based model invariant to monotonic feature transforms — z-score shifts/scales features but preserves relative ordering, so split thresholds adapt automatically. Prior independent verification (GPT-2 XL 0.13→0.68, OLMo 0.25→0.83) was with a scale-sensitive classifier (LR), not RF. Raw results match cross_strategy_loo_001 exactly (mean acc=0.5908). Non-temp09 impact negligible (mean Δd1v2=+0.006).
